In this context, a key strategic goal of the ministry is to increase the industrial sector's share in growth based on efficiency growth in the coming period. These goals were included in the Turkish Industrial Strategy Document, which was co-ordinated in the ministry's co-ordination, covering 2011-2014. The document determines a long-term vision, general goals, and strategic goals. The document contains a 72-point action plan. Turkey's Industrial Strategy also includes other strategy documents prepared by the ministry. It is possible to set them up as a strategy document and action plan for the Turkish machine industry, automotive sector, ceramic sector, chemical sector, electricity and electronic sector, iron steel and non-iron metal sector. Under the remembered law, the Technological Development Support Programme is under way to promote qualified entrepreneurial initiatives, to create companies that can develop innovative competitiveness and technological levels of high-profile products and processes, as well as raising awareness of information in our country about intensive or innovative entrepreneurial initiatives, and providing support for young entrepreneurs with no guarantees up to 100,000 euro. The main goal of our defence policy, based on international law and the country's defence needs, is to protect national independence, sovereignty, territorial integrity, and vital interests. We are also contributing with one corvet to the Marine Task Force of the United Nations Temporary Task Force in Lebanon. tbmm-2012-12-15sit02spe0141par0356	Mr. President, members of lawmakers, I have taken a personal oath on this part of the 2013 budget. I'll give you my opinion on the national Defense Department budget. I salute the General Assembly. In our country, as in many countries in the world after the Cold War, there is a need to review the concepts of defence and therefore the organisation of the armed forces and their possibilities. However, the restructuring of our country for national defence has not yet been implemented, and it is late in the process. Turkey, on the other hand, is at the head of countries that need this reform most because of the internal dynamics it has, and, unfortunately, the external dynamics that are so far ahead of us these days. This requirement for change and development is mainly focused on three areas: the structure of our army, increased defence capabilities and modernisation based on high technology in the defence industry. In these areas, mandatory distances will strengthen the defence of the country and strengthen Turkey's hand in foreign policy. I must point out right away that the idea that we have a strong hand here is that it will contribute more internationally to our ability to have more say-so, and it will never express its desire to sail into adventurous waters, as the AKP did.
